=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
Find the number, modulo 998244353, of permutations P=(P_1,P_2,\cdots,P_N) of (1,2,\cdots,N) that satisfy all of the following M conditions.

- The i-th condition: The maximum among P_{L_i},P_{L_i+1},\cdots,P_{R_i} is not P_{X_i}.
Here, L_i, R_i, and X_i are integers given in the input.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N M
L_1 R_1 X_1
L_2 R_2 X_2
\vdots
L_M R_M X_M

Output

Print the answer.

Constraints


- 1 \leq N \leq 500
- 1 \leq M \leq 10^5
- 1 \leq L_i \leq X_i \leq R_i \leq N
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

3 2
1 3 2
1 2 1

Sample Output 1

1

Only one permutation, P=(1,2,3), satisfies the conditions.

Sample Input 2

5 1
1 1 1

Sample Output 2

0

Sample Input 3

10 5
3 8 4
3 10 4
1 7 2
1 8 3
3 8 7

Sample Output 3

1598400

Sample Input 4

15 17
2 11 9
2 15 13
1 14 2
5 11 5
3 15 11
1 6 2
4 15 12
3 11 6
9 13 10
2 14 6
10 15 11
1 8 6
6 14 8
2 10 2
6 12 6
3 14 12
2 6 2

Sample Output 4

921467228

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's deeply verify DP invariant.

Define dp[l][r] = number of relative permutations (orders) of positions l..r such that for every condition (L,R,X) with l <= L <= R <= r, the maximum of subarray [L,R] is not X. Here "relative permutation" means assign distinct values to positions l..r; count relative order, not actual values. Since conditions only depend on relative order, and any relative order can be realized by exactly one assignment of any chosen subset values? For fixed interval [l,r] in original positions, if we consider only positions l..r, their relative order is a permutation of length len. For each such relative order, there are C(N,len) ways to choose actual values? But DP counts relative orders. The recurrence with binomial factor counts relative orders of full interval from relative orders of left/right and choice of subset of lower values. Let's verify.

For interval [l,r], root k is position with maximum value among l..r. In relative order, root has rank len (largest). The left positions have some relative order of length a, right length b. The values assigned to left/right are a subset of {1,...,len-1} of size a for left; this choice determines interleaving of ranks between left and right. Number of such choices C(len-1,a). For each choice, combine left relative order (renumbered by chosen subset) and right relative order (complement). This yields a unique relative order of [l,r] with root k. Conversely any relative order with root k gives left/right relative orders and subset. So recurrence correct.

Conditions:
- If a condition's interval [L,R] is contained in [l,r] and contains k, then since k is max over [l,r], k is max over [L,R]. If X=k, condition fails; if X != k, condition holds automatically (max is k not X). We allow k only if no condition with X=k and [L,R] contained in [l,r]. Our badR check exactly ensures no such condition. If X != k, no need to check because max is k, and condition says max != X; since X != k, satisfied. What if X not in [L,R]? Input guarantees X in [L,R], so if contains k and X != k, X is some other position in [L,R], max k != X, satisfied.
- If condition's interval [L,R] is contained in [l,r] and does not contain k, then it lies entirely in left or right subinterval. It is counted in dp[left] or dp[right] by induction.
- If condition's interval is not contained in [l,r], it is not considered at this dp; it will be considered at larger intervals. At final [1,N], all conditions contained. Good.

Need check badR check with "condition with X=k and [L,R] contained in [l,r]" exactly bad[k][l] <= r. Because condition has L>=l and R<=r. Since L<=X=k and R>=k. For fixed k, l, bad[k][l] = min R among all conditions with X=k and L>=l. If min R <= r, there exists condition with L>=l and R<=r. But does it also satisfy L<=k? Input yes. And R>=k. Since r>=k (k in interval), R<=r. So contained. Conversely if any contained condition, its R <= r and L>=l, so minR <= R <= r. Good. So allowed iff bad[k][l] > r.

But wait: bad[k][l] computed as min R among conditions with X=k and L>=l. If there is a condition with L>=l but L > r? But L<=k<=r, so L<=r. Fine. If condition L>=l and R<=r, then L<=k<=R<=r, so L<=r. Good.

Need handle conditions with L=R=X: For k=X, direct_bad[X][X] = X (R=X). Then bad[X][l] for l<=X <= X. For interval [l,r] containing X, if r>=X, bad[X][l] <= X <= r, so blocked. Thus any interval containing X cannot have root X. In particular dp[X][X] root X blocked -> 0. This makes answer 0 if any singleton condition? Wait if condition L=R=X, then for interval [X,X], root X blocked, dp=0. Since final dp includes that subproblem? Yes dp[1][N] recurrence will multiply by dp[X][X] for some root choices? If dp[X][X]=0, then any full permutation where the subinterval [X,X] appears as a subproblem? In recurrence, dp[X][X] is used whenever root splits such that X isolated. But is it always used? For final dp[1][N], the interval [X,X] is a subproblem in the recursive decomposition only if at some ancestor, root is outside [X,X] and the interval containing X eventually becomes [X,X]. In any Cartesian tree, each singleton interval is a leaf. The DP for [1,N] sums over all trees; the leaf dp[X][X] is multiplied. If dp[X][X]=0, total 0. Is that correct? If condition L=R=X, max of singleton is X, impossible, so answer 0. Good.

Now, is it possible that condition with X=k and interval [L,R] contained in [l,r] but root k of [l,r] is not max over [L,R] because there is another position in [L,R] with larger value outside [l,r]? No, [L,R] subset [l,r], and k is max over [l,r], so k max over [L,R]. Good.

Thus DP is correct.

Let's consider alternative interpretation: The condition "The maximum among P_L..P_R is not P_X" means P_X is not the maximum value in that interval. Since P is permutation, if X is not max, there is some other position with greater value. Our root max approach handles.

But what if X is max over [L,R] but not root of any interval that contains [L,R]? In Cartesian tree, max over [L,R] is the LCA of L,R, which is root of the minimal interval? Actually it is a node in the tree; it is root of the subtree spanning [L,R]? The maximum of [L,R] is the root of the Cartesian tree of subarray [L,R], which is the LCA of L and R. This node is also root of some interval [a,b] in the recursive decomposition where [a,b] is the span of that node's subtree, which contains [L,R]. So if X is max over [L,R], then in DP, when we choose root X for interval [a,b] (the subtree span of X), the condition interval [L,R] is contained in [a,b] and contains X, so blocked. Conversely if X not max over [L,R], then no ancestor interval containing [L,R] has root X? Wait X could be root of some larger interval that contains [L,R] but not all? If X is root of interval [a,b] containing [L,R], then X is max over [a,b], hence max over [L,R] because X∈[L,R]. So impossible. Thus blocking root X for any interval containing a condition with X exactly prevents X being max over that condition. Good.

Now, maybe there is a condition with X=k but interval [L,R] not contained in current [l,r]; root k of [l,r] could still be max over [L,R] if [l,r] is not the full subtree span? Wait if [l,r] is a subproblem interval in the recursive decomposition, it corresponds to a subtree of some node? Actually in DP, intervals [l,r] are arbitrary contiguous subarrays, not necessarily subtree spans? In recurrence, every interval [l,r] considered is a subarray whose root is max; it is exactly the span of a subtree in the Cartesian tree for that subarray? For the full array, recursive intervals are subtree spans. But DP also computes dp for all intervals, including those that may not be subtree spans in a particular full tree. However dp[l][r] counts relative orders of positions l..r considered as an isolated array; in the full tree, if [l,r] is not a subtree span, its internal max is still the LCA of l,r? Actually subtree span of LCA(l,r) is [a,b] containing [l,r], possibly larger). The DP recurrence for dp[l][r] chooses root k as max over [l,r], and then recursively left/right. This is exactly building the Cartesian tree of the subarray [l,r] as an isolated array, not necessarily equal to the induced subtree of full tree? But the Cartesian tree of a subarray is the same as the full Cartesian tree restricted to that subarray? I think yes: The maximum of [l,r] is the LCA of l,r in full tree; its left subtree within [l,r] is the Cartesian tree of [l,k-1], etc. This is the "Cartesian tree of subarray" which is the minimal subtree connecting l..r? Actually for any interval [l,r], the recursive max decomposition is valid and yields a binary tree that is the Cartesian tree of the subarray. In the full tree, the root k may have ancestors outside [l,r] with larger values, but within [l,r] it is max. The subtree span of k in full tree may include positions outside [l,r]? Can a node's subtree span extend outside [l,r] if k is max of [l,r]? Suppose full array [1,5], values: pos3=10, pos2=9, pos1=8, pos4=7,pos5=6. Interval [2,4] max pos3, subtree span of pos3 is [1,5] because pos3 root of whole. So k's full subtree span [1,5] contains [2,4]. In isolated dp[2,4], root k=3, left [2,2], right [4,4]. This is not the full subtree of k (which includes pos1,pos5 as descendants? Actually in full Cartesian tree, pos1 and pos5 are descendants of pos3? Let's build: root pos3, left subtree positions1-2 with root pos2, left pos1; right subtree positions4-5 root pos4, right pos5. So pos1 and pos5 are descendants of pos3. The isolated subarray [2,4] Cartesian tree root pos3, left pos2, right pos4. It is the subtree of pos3 restricted to [2,4], not including pos1,pos5. So dp[l][r] counts relative orders of subarray, not necessarily full subtree. Conditions whose interval [L,R] is contained in [l,r] but not in the full subtree? They are considered. Conditions with interval extending outside [l,r] are not considered in dp[l][r], but they may involve root k and be affected by values outside [l,r]. In the full permutation, if k is max over [l,r] but there are larger values outside [l,r], then for a condition interval [L,R] that contains k and extends outside [l,r], the max over [L,R] might be outside, not k. So no violation due to k. Our DP final full [1,N] will check all conditions at appropriate intervals. The recurrence for subinterval dp[l][r] intentionally ignores conditions not contained in [l,r] because they depend on outside values. This is standard.

But wait: In the recurrence for dp[l][r], when choosing root k and combining left/right, we multiply by dp[left] and dp[right] which count relative orders satisfying conditions contained in left/right. But what about conditions contained in [l,r] that contain k and have X != k? They are automatically satisfied because max over [l,r] is k. What about conditions contained in [l,r] that contain k and have X=k? They are blocked by bad. Good. What about conditions contained in [l,r] that do not contain k? They are in left or right and handled. So dp[l][r] exactly counts relative orders of subarray satisfying all conditions contained in [l,r]. This invariant is independent of outside values. Good.

Now, is the binomial factor C(len-1, left_size) still correct when dp[left] and dp[right] count relative orders satisfying conditions contained in left/right, but conditions involving positions in left and right (crossing k) are automatically satisfied if X != k and blocked if X=k. What about conditions contained in [l,r] that contain k and X != k, but X could be in left or right; max is k, so condition satisfied regardless of relative order of left/right. Good. Conditions that contain k and X=k blocked. Conditions that contain k but X? Input X in interval, so if X != k, X is in left/right. Good.

Thus DP correct.

Implementation:

import sys
MOD = 998244353

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data: return
    it = iter(data)
    N = next(it); M = next(it)
    INF = N + 1
    bad = [[INF]*(N+2) for _ in range(N+2)]
    for _ in range(M):
        L = next(it); R = next(it); X = next(it)
        if R < bad[X][L]:
            bad[X][L] = R
    # suffix minima
    for k in range(1, N+1):
        row = bad[k]
        # for l from k-1 down to 1
        for l in range(k-1, 0, -1):
            if row[l+1] < row[l]:
                row[l] = row[l+1]
    # combinations
    C = [[0]*(N+1) for _ in range(N+1)]
    for n in range(N+1):
        C[n][0] = C[n][n] = 1
        for k in range(1, n):
            C[n][k] = (C[n-1][k-1] + C[n-1][k]) % MOD
    dp = [[0]*(N+2) for _ in range(N+2)]
    for i in range(1, N+2):
        dp[i][i-1] = 1
    # DP
    for length in range(1, N+1):
        comb_len = C[length-1]
        for l in range(1, N-length+2):
            r = l + length - 1
            total = 0
            # maybe local dp, bad
            for k in range(l, r+1):
                if bad[k][l] <= r:
                    continue
                term = comb_len[k-l] * dp[l][k-1] % MOD
                term = term * dp[k+1][r] % MOD
                total += term
            dp[l][r] = total % MOD
    print(dp[1][N] % MOD)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()

Now, let's think about if dp[l][r] for l>r not set for all, but dp[r+1][r] set. For right empty when k=r, dp[k+1][r] = dp[r+1][r] set. For left empty when k=l, dp[l][l-1] set. Good.

Let's think about mathematical correctness in more detail, maybe there is a subtlety with relative orders and binomial factor when conditions are not "subtree" but arbitrary subarray. Let's prove recurrence by considering any relative permutation Q of positions l..r. Let k be position of maximum value (rank len). Then Q restricted to left [l,k-1] is a relative permutation counted by dp[l][k-1]? Does it satisfy all conditions contained in left? Conditions contained in left are contained in [l,r] and do not contain k, so their max in full Q is same as max in left relative order. Since Q satisfies them, left restriction satisfies. Similarly right. Conversely, given left/right relative orders satisfying their contained conditions, and a choice of subset S of {1..len-1} for left values, construct Q. Need show all conditions contained in [l,r] are satisfied:
- Conditions contained in left/right: satisfied by induction.
- Conditions containing k: Since k has max value in [l,r], max of any such interval is k. If X != k, condition satisfied. If X=k, such condition would make k blocked, but we only allow k if bad[k][l] > r, which means no condition with X=k and interval contained in [l,r]. Thus no violating condition. So all satisfied.
This is a bijection. Good.

Now, let's think if a condition contained in [l,r] with X=k but interval not containing k? Impossible because X in interval and X=k, so contains k. Good.

Now, let's consider if bad[k][l] > r exactly no condition with X=k and interval contained in [l,r]. We argued. Let's prove:
There exists condition (L,R,k) contained in [l,r] iff ∃ condition with L>=l and R<=r. Since L<=k<=R. For fixed k,l, define B = min R among conditions with L>=l. If B <= r, then the condition attaining B has R=B<=r and L>=l, so contained. If no condition with R<=r, then all R>r, so B>r. Good.

Potential optimizations:
- Use local variables for dp, bad, C, MOD.
- In inner loop, avoid dp[l][k-1] row lookup repeated? Could store left_dp = dp[l] and right? dp[k+1][r] row varies. Maybe okay.
- Use `for k in range(l, r + 1):` and `if bad[k][l] <= r: continue`.
- Access `bad_k = bad[k]`? Since bad[k][l] row. We can do `if bad_k[l] <= r`. But bad_k changes with k.
- Use `dp_l = dp[l]` for left, but dp[l][k-1] uses dp_l. For right, `dp[k+1][r]` row. Could use `dp_rows = dp` local.
- Use `comb = comb_len[k-l]`.
- Maybe precompute for each l and r the list of allowed k? Not necessary.
- Could use 1-indexed arrays and local variables.

Let's write optimized inner loop:
for length in range(1, N+1):
    comb_len = C[length-1]
    for l in range(1, N-length+2):
        r = l + length - 1
        total = 0
        dp_l = dp[l]
        for k in range(l, r+1):
            if bad[k][l] <= r:
                continue
            term = comb_len[k-l] * dp_l[k-1] % MOD
            term = term * dp[k+1][r] % MOD
            total += term
        dp[l][r] = total % MOD

Here dp_l[k-1] for k=l uses dp_l[l-1] set. Good. dp[k+1][r] for k=r uses dp[r+1][r] set. Good.

Could also precompute `bad_col_l`? bad[k][l] for fixed l varies k. We can't easily.

Maybe use list of lists and local `bad_local = bad`, `dp_local = dp`, `mod = MOD`.

Potential issue: `dp_l = dp[l]` but dp[l][r] assignment after loop; dp_l is row l, so dp_l[r] = total % MOD. Good.

Could use `for k, bad_k in enumerate(bad[l:r+1], start=l)`? But bad rows not contiguous? bad is list by k, so bad[k][l]. Slicing creates list, not good.

Could precompute `allowed` boolean? Not needed.

Now, let's think about if we can reduce modulo operations: term = comb_len[k-l] * dp_l[k-1] % MOD; term = term * dp[k+1][r] % MOD. This is two mod per allowed k. Number allowed maybe less. 21M*2 mod operations okay. Could do `term = (comb_len[k-l] * dp_l[k-1] * dp[k+1][r]) % MOD` but product up to 1e27, Python big int but still okay; two mods maybe faster? Now, let's think about if there is a condition with interval [L,R] and X=k, and k is root of [l,r] where [L,R] subset [l,r] but [l,r] is not a subproblem in the recursive decomposition of the full tree? In DP, every interval considered is a possible subproblem. If a full permutation has k as max over [l,r], then in the recursive decomposition, [l,r] might not appear as a subproblem if [l,r] is not a subtree span? Actually the recursive decomposition of the full array considers intervals that are subtree spans of nodes. If k is max over [l,r] but there are larger values outside [l,r], then [l,r] is not a subtree span of k; k's subtree span is larger. However [l,r] may appear as a subproblem if the root of a larger interval is outside [l,r] and splits such that [l,r] is one side? Let's examine. Full array [1,5], root=1, right [2,5], root=5, left [2,4], root=3. Here interval [2,4] is a subproblem (subtree span of 3). Suppose k=3 is root of [2,4]. Now, what if k is max over [l,r] but [l,r] is not a subproblem in the decomposition? In any full Cartesian tree, every node's subtree span is an interval that appears as a subproblem. If k is max over an arbitrary interval [l,r], then [l,r] is the intersection of k's subtree span with some interval? But DP counts by considering all possible subproblems; the recurrence for a larger interval uses dp for subintervals that are exactly the left/right parts after removing root. Those subintervals are subtree spans of the roots of those parts. Thus every interval that appears as a subproblem is a subtree span of some node in the constructed tree. If k is max over [l,r] but [l,r] is not a subtree span, then in the constructed tree, k's subtree span is larger; [l,r] may not appear as a subproblem. Does DP need to block k as root of [l,r]? It won't because dp[l][r] may not be used in that full tree. But if [l,r] is not a subproblem, the condition [L,R] subset [l,r] and X=k will be handled at k's actual subtree span (larger), which contains [l,r] and thus contains [L,R]. There, root k will be blocked. So no need to block at [l,r]. Our DP computes dp[l][r] for all intervals, but only uses some in a given full tree. Blocking k in dp[l][r] for all intervals containing [L,R] is actually more restrictive than necessary? Wait, if [l,r] is not a subproblem in a particular full tree, dp[l][r] not used. But dp[l][r] is a count of relative orders of that subarray considered in isolation. If a relative order of [l,r] has k as max and [L,R] subset [l,r] with X=k, then in isolation it violates the condition. But in a full permutation where [l,r] is not a subtree span, could the same relative order of [l,r] be part of a valid full permutation? Yes, if there are larger values outside [l,r] that make max over [L,R] not k? Wait if [L,R] subset [l,r] and k is max over [l,r], then k is max over [L,R] regardless of outside values because [L,R] contains only positions in [l,r]. So if condition [L,R] X=k is contained in [l,r], then any full permutation where the relative order of [l,r] has k as max will violate that condition, no matter what outside values are. Therefore such relative order of [l,r] is invalid globally. So dp[l][r] should exclude it. Thus blocking k for all intervals containing [L,R] is correct, even if [l,r] not a subtree span. Because if k is max over [l,r], then k is max over [L,R]. Good.

Now, let's think about if a condition [L,R] X=k is contained in [l,r], but k is not max over [l,r]; then the relative order of [l,r] may still have k as max over [L,R]? In that case, in the relative order of [l,r], k is not max over [l,r], so dp[l][r] would not choose k as root of [l,r]. But does this also determine the interleaving of left and right in the full relative order? Yes, because left positions all have values from chosen subset, right from complement. The relative order within each side is fixed. The full relative order is determined by the actual values assigned to each position. There is no additional shuffle factor because the positions are fixed; the values determine order. For example, left relative order [a1<a2], right [b1<b2], choose subset {1,3} for left, complement {2,4} for right. Then full order is a1(1), b1(2), a2(3), b2(4). This is one full relative order. If choose subset {1,2} for left, full order a1(1), a2(2), b1(3), b2(4). Different. The binomial factor counts all such interleavings. Good.

Let's write final code:

import sys
MOD = 998244353

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    N, M = data[0], data[1]
    INF = N + 1
    bad = [[INF] * (N + 2) for _ in range(N + 2)]
    idx = 2
    for _ in range(M):
        L = data[idx]; R = data[idx+1]; X = data[idx+2]; idx += 3
        if R < bad[X][L]:
            bad[X][L] = R

    for k in range(1, N + 1):
        row = bad[k]
        # row[l] = min R among conditions with X=k and L>=l
        for l in range(k - 1, 0, -1):
            if row[l + 1] < row[l]:
                row[l] = row[l + 1]

    # transpose for faster access: bad_cols[l][k] = bad[k][l]
    bad_cols = [[bad[k][l] for k in range(N + 2)] for l in range(N + 2)]

    # combinations
    C = [[0] * (N + 1) for _ in range(N + 1)]
    for n in range(N + 1):
        C[n][0] = 1
        C[n][n] = 1
        for k in range(1, n):
            C[n][k] = (C[n-1][k-1] + C[n-1][k]) % MOD

    dp = [[0] * (N + 2) for _ in range(N + 2)]
    for i in range(1, N + 2):
        dp[i][i - 1] = 1

    mod = MOD
    for length in range(1, N + 1):
        comb_len = C[length - 1]
        for l in range(1, N - length + 2):
            r = l + length - 1
            total = 0
            dp_l = dp[l]
            bad_l = bad_cols[l]
            for k in range(l, r + 1):
                if bad_l[k] <= r:
                    continue
                total += comb_len[k - l] * dp_l[k - 1] * dp[k + 1][r]
            dp_l[r] = total % mod

    print(dp[1][N] % mod)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
